\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}} }=\frac{\Lambda^{2}}{k}+2\Lambda+\frac{k}{6}(-5+6\log\frac{k}{\Lambda})~.\end{split}

(88)

Due to the presence of the cut-off
\Lambda
the answer does not satisfy the conformal ward identity (CWI). This can be remedied by using a alternate regularization scheme,
analytic regularization
introduced in
[
18
]
. However, for this particular example it is possible to convert this answer to something that does satisfy the CWI at 2-pts by removing the polynomial divergence in
\Lambda
and setting
\Lambda=k
.

(89)

where
\kappa
is the regulator that arises by replacing the divergent part of the answer in hard-cutoff,
\log(\frac{k}{\Lambda})
, by
\frac{1}{\kappa}
. Note that in this regularization scheme, the flat space limit is zero, which is consistent with the loop integral in flat space when evaluated in analytic regularization
[
82
]
.

5.2
Triangle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{14.47264pt}{8.77808pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 20.03 12.15)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.86693pt}{-15.40694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -9.5 -21.32)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-28.2065pt}{8.77808pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -39.03 12.15)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}} }\end{split}

(90)

which when written explicitly,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{2}dz_{3}e^{-x_{1}z_{1}}e^{-x_{2}z_{2}}e^{-x_{3}z_{3}}\mbox{tr}\big[S(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{y}_{12})S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{y}_{23})S(z_{3},z_{1},\vec{y}_{31})\big]~.\end{split}

(91)

We use the recursion relations derived in section
3
and write the
z
-integral as a sum of two contributions: “Bulk” (denoted by
\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}_{B}
) and “Boundary” (denoted by
\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}_{\partial}
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}=\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}_{\partial}+\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}_{B}~.\end{split}

(92)

Due to the cyclic structure of the integral (
91
), there is a permutation symmetry in both the terms. The boundary term is given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}_{\partial}&=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{2}dz_{3}e^{-x_{2}z_{2}}e^{-x_{3}z_{3}}\mbox{tr}\big[S(0,z_{2},\vec{y}_{12})S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{y}_{23})S(z_{3},0,\vec{y}_{31})\big]+\mbox{perms}~,\end{split}

(93)

where perms denote the permutation cycle
(x_{2},x_{3},x_{1})(\vec{y}_{12},\vec{y}_{23},\vec{y}_{31})
. Using (
46
) the trace evaluates to zero

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mbox{tr}\big[S(0,z_{2},\vec{y}_{12})S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{y}_{23})S(z_{3},0,\vec{y}_{31})\big]=\mbox{tr}\big[P_{+}\Pi_{2}(\vec{y}_{12})S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{y}_{23})\Pi_{2}(\vec{y}_{31})P_{-}\big]\\
&=\mbox{tr}\big[P_{-}P_{+}\Pi_{2}(\vec{y}_{12})S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{y}_{23})\Pi_{2}(\vec{y}_{31})\big]=0~,\end{split}

(94)

resulting in

\displaystyle\begin{split}\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}_{\partial}=0~.\end{split}

(95)

This simplification occurs for any one-loop polygon with Yukawa interaction and more generally for any interaction vertex that is proportional to the identity matrix or
\gamma^{z}
. The bulk term
\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}_{B}
does not apriori receive any such simplification and simplifies to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}_{B}&=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{2}dz_{3}e^{-(x_{1}+y_{12})z_{1}}e^{-(x_{2}+y_{23})z_{2}}e^{-x_{3}z_{3}}\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\mbox{tr}\Big[(y_{12}+i\gamma^{z}\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{y}_{12})S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{y}_{23})S(z_{3},z_{1},\vec{y}_{31})\Big]+\mbox{perms.}\end{split}

(96)

where perms. denotes cycling between
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})(\vec{y}_{12},\vec{y}_{23},\vec{y}_{31})
.
Therefore the
z
-integrals reduce to the tree level 5-pt function (
55
). For this particular case, it is simple enough to evaluate the integrals and traces at this stage directly. The two kinds of traces that appear in (
96
) are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mbox{tr}\Big[(\gamma^{z}+i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{p}_{1})(\gamma^{z}+i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{p}_{2})\Big]&=4(1-\vec{p}_{1}\cdot\vec{p}_{2}),\\
\mbox{tr}\Big[(i\gamma^{z}\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{p}_{1})(\gamma^{z}+i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{p}_{2})(\gamma^{z}+i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{p}_{3})\Big]&=4\vec{p}_{1}\cdot(\vec{p}_{3}-\vec{p}_{2}).\end{split}

(97)

This leaves us with four
z
-integrals in (
96
) which are given as,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{1}&=\frac{1}{\left(x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{2}^{\prime}+x_{3}\right)\left(y_{31}+x_{2}^{\prime}+x_{3}\right)\left(y_{23}+x_{2}^{\prime}\right)},\\
\mathcal{I}_{2}&=\frac{1}{\left(x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{2}^{\prime}+x_{3}\right)\left(y_{31}+x_{1}^{\prime}\right)\left(y_{23}+x_{2}^{\prime}\right)},\\
\mathcal{I}_{3}&=\frac{y_{31}+y_{23}+x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{2}^{\prime}+2x_{3}}{\left(x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{2}^{\prime}+x_{3}\right)\left(y_{31}+x_{2}^{\prime}+x_{3}\right)\left(y_{23}+x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{3}\right)\left(y_{31}+y_{23}+x_{3}\right)},\\
\mathcal{I}_{4}&=\frac{1}{\left(x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{2}^{\prime}+x_{3}\right)\left(y_{31}+x_{1}^{\prime}\right)\left(y_{23}+x_{1}^{\prime}+x_{3}\right)}\end{split}

(98)

where
x_{1}^{\prime}=x_{1}+y_{12},\ x_{2}^{\prime}=x_{2}+y_{12}
. Summing everything up we obtain the full answer,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\scalebox{1.5}{$\Delta$}&=\frac{4}{x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}}\Bigg[y_{12}\Big\{(\mathcal{I}_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{4})(1-\frac{\vec{y}_{23}\cdot\vec{y}_{31}}{y_{23}y_{31}})-(\mathcal{I}_{2}+\mathcal{I}_{3})(1+\frac{\vec{y}_{23}\cdot\vec{y}_{31}}{y_{23}y_{31}})\Big\}\\
&\qquad+\vec{y}_{12}\cdot\Big\{-(\mathcal{I}_{1}-\mathcal{I}_{4})(\frac{\vec{y}_{31}}{y_{31}}-\frac{\vec{y}_{23}}{y_{23}})+(\mathcal{I}_{2}-\mathcal{I}_{3})(\frac{\vec{y}_{31}}{y_{31}}+\frac{\vec{y}_{23}}{y_{23}})\Big\}\Bigg]+\mbox{perms}~.\end{split}

(99)

A brute force computation of the same diagram results in summing over 100 individual terms without any apriori pattern! This example shows how the recursion relations combine the result of radial integrals along with the trace of the
\gamma
-matrices into a simple form. By comparing with the integrand of the scalar triangle graph (see equation 3.29 of
[
17
]
) we see that the poles of the fermionic triangle are the same as the scalar triangle and hence for odd-gons, there is no simplification in the transcendentality of the loop integral. Unfortunately, the tools used in the previous section are not useful for performing this loop integral as it is no longer axi-symmetric. While it is possible to express this integral as an infinite series by expanding around the squeezed limit of two momenta as done for the scalar triangle in section 3.2.2 of
[
17
]
, the summation does not yield a closed form expression. However, given that this is a 3-pt function and should ultimately be constrained by conformal invariance, we expect there should be a closed form answer for this integral and we leave a detailed investigation of this integral to a future project.

Note that the recursion relation can also be used for QED in order to write a compact form for the diagram that contributes to the triangle anomaly in flat space,

(101)

where
\Pi_{1}(\vec{y})
and
\Pi_{2}(\vec{y})
are defined in (
50
). While this is sufficient to evaluate the loop integrand, performing this loop integral is an arduous task and it is not yet known how one can perform the integral explicitly. We hope to address this point in the future.

In the following section, we explain how the 1-loop integrands can be obtained via tree-level amplitudes by an alternate method.

5.3
Loops from Trees

It is also possible to obtain the one-loop integrand at
n
-pts by using the tree-level graph at
(n+1)
-pts. This is often known as the
tree theorem

[
83
,
84
,
85
]
and was explored for the wave function of the universe in dS in
[
49
]
which are equivalent to the correlators in EAdS. These were also recently extended to cosmological correlators in
[
50
]
. In this section we demonstrate that a treatment similar to
[
49
]
also applies to diagrams with fermion exchange.

Consider any general tree-level Witten diagram with massless fermionic exchanges of the form,

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}&=\int dz_{1}\cdots dz_{n}e^{-x_{1}z_{1}}V_{1}S(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{y}_{12})V_{2}e^{-x_{2}z_{2}}V_{2}^{\prime}S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{y}_{23})V_{3}\\
&\qquad\times\cdots e^{-x_{n-1}z_{n-1}}V^{\prime}_{n-1}S(z_{n-1},z_{n},\vec{y}_{n-1n})V_{n}e^{-x_{n}z_{n}}\end{split}

(102)

where
V_{i}
’s are factors appearing through interaction vertices. By deforming two of the
x
’s via
x_{j_{1}}\to x_{j_{1}}+\epsilon
and
x_{j_{3}}\to x_{j_{3}}-\epsilon
and integrating the resulting function in
\epsilon
over the contour from
(-i\infty,+i\infty)
we get,

\displaystyle\int dz_{1}\cdots dz_{n}e^{-x_{1}z_{1}}V_{1}S(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{y}_{12})V_{2}e^{-x_{2}z_{2}}V_{2}^{\prime}S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{y}_{23})V_{3}

(103)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\cdots\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\epsilon}{2\pi i}e^{-\epsilon(z_{j_{1}}-z_{j_{3}})}V_{j_{1}}S(z_{j_{1}},z_{j_{2}},\vec{y}_{j_{1}j_{2}})V_{j_{2}}e^{-x_{j_{2}}z_{j_{2}}}V_{j_{2}}^{\prime}S(z_{j_{2}},z_{j_{3}},\vec{y}_{j_{2}j_{3}})V_{j_{3}}\cdots

\displaystyle\qquad\times\cdots e^{-x_{n-1}z_{n-1}}V^{\prime}_{n-1}S(z_{n-1},z_{n},\vec{y}_{n-1n})V_{n}e^{-x_{n}z_{n}}~.

This sets
z_{j_{1}}=z_{j_{3}}
and thus, provides the following one-loop integrand from the tree-level diagram,

By deforming a different set of
x_{j}
’s it is possible to obtain other one-loop diagrams. The nice property of this representation is that it can also be performed after doing the
z
-integrals, which results in summing over residues in the
\epsilon
-plane. Therefore, this version of the tree theorem for Witten diagrams
[
49
]
also applies to half-integer spins. Although this should also work for massive fermions one needs to exercise caution when there are IR divergences as the integrals in
z
and
\epsilon
do not always commute. In appendix
C
we provide examples of using the tree theorem to evaluate the loop integrand with massive internal legs for cases without IR divergences.

6
Massive Fermions and Weight Shifting Operators

In this section, we explain how the recursion relations developed in section
3
also generalize to massive fermions in AdS. We first consider the case of massive fermions in the external legs.

6.1
External Legs

The massive fermion bulk-boundary propagator (for an outgoing leg
\psi
of mass
m
) is
[
59
,
53
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}^{m}(z,\vec{k})=z^{2}\Big(K_{m+\frac{1}{2}}(kz)+i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}K_{m-\frac{1}{2}}(kz)\Big)~,\end{split}

(104)

where we have suppressed the dependence on the boundary spinor
\chi
for brevity. For
m=0
this reduces to (
6
). For the case
m=1
this becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}^{m=1}(z,\vec{k})=z^{2}\Big(K_{\frac{3}{2}}(kz)+i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}K_{\frac{1}{2}}(kz)\Big)~.\end{split}

(105)

One can now use the following relation for
K_{3/2}(x)

\displaystyle\begin{split}K_{3/2}(kz)&=-\frac{\sqrt{k}}{z}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}K_{1/2}(kz)\right)=-\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sqrt{k}}{z^{3/2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\left(\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}\right)~.\end{split}

(106)

Using this we write
\mathcal{B}^{m=1}(z,\vec{k})
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}^{m=1}(z,\vec{k})&=z^{2}\Bigg(i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{e^{-kz}}{\sqrt{kz}}-\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{\sqrt{k}}{z^{3/2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\left(\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}\right)\Bigg)\\
&=-\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{z}{k}}\Bigg\{i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}+(i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}+k)\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\Bigg\}\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}~.\end{split}

(107)

Hence for any diagram with a fermion of
m=1
as an external leg, we can define the following differential operator
\hat{D}_{k}
to map it to a conformally coupled case (it will be convenient to express in terms of a left-handed derivative)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{B}^{m=1}(z,\vec{k})=\sqrt{z}\hat{D}_{k}\frac{e^{-kz}}{k},\qquad\hat{D}_{k}=-\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\Bigg\{i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}+(i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}+k)\overset{\leftarrow}{\frac{\partial}{\partial k}}\Bigg\}~.\end{split}

(108)

The corresponding equation for massive ingoing
\bar{\psi}
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{m=1}(z,\vec{k})=\sqrt{z}\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}\hat{\bar{D}}_{k},\qquad\hat{\bar{D}}_{k}=-\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}\Bigg\{i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}+(i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}+k)\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\Bigg\}~.\end{split}

(109)

These are analogous to
Weight shifting operators
for half-integer spin in momentum space
[
86
,
87
,
39
,
88
,
89
]
. While these differential operators are trivially used for Witten diagrams at 4-pts, one needs to exercise caution while using the recursion relations along with the differential operators at higher points. This is because additional tensors are obtained from the differential operators above for fermions emanating from internal vertices at higher point graphs.

To demonstrate its use we consider a 3-pt function in Yukawa theory of a conformally coupled scalar and fermions of
m=1
, then we get,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-32.79414pt}{-20.03853pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -45.38 -27.73)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-32.79414pt}{15.52742pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -45.38 21.49)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{33.57289pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 46.45 -3.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dz}{z^{4}}\Big(\sqrt{z}\hat{\bar{D}}_{k_{1}}\frac{e^{-k_{2}z}}{k_{2}}\Big)\Big(ze^{-k_{3}z}\Big)\Big(\sqrt{z}\hat{\bar{D}}_{k_{2}}\frac{e^{-k_{1}z}}{k_{1}}\Big)\\
&=\hat{D}_{k_{1}}\frac{1}{k_{1}k_{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dz}{z^{2}}e^{-k_{123}z}\hat{\bar{D}}_{k_{2}}\\
&=\lim_{z_{*}\to 0}\hat{D}_{k_{1}}\Bigg[\frac{1}{k_{1}k_{2}}\left\{\frac{1}{z_{*}}+k_{123}\log(k_{123}z_{*})+(\gamma_{E}-1)k_{123}\right\}\Bigg]\hat{\bar{D}}_{k_{2}}~,\end{split}

(110)

where we have denoted the IR cutoff of the
z-
integral by
z_{*}
and
\gamma_{E}
is the Euler–Mascheroni constant. The IR divergences are discussed in more detail in the following section
6.3
. Note that in order to use the differential operators, momentum conservation at the vertex must be imposed after taking the derivative. Diagrams with a scalar exchange and external fermions can be evaluated in a similar manner. For example,

4
3
2
1

\displaystyle=\hat{D}_{k_{2}}\hat{D}_{k_{4}}\frac{1}{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}^{2}}\frac{dz_{2}}{z_{2}^{2}}e^{-k_{12}z_{1}}G_{D}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})e^{-k_{34}z_{2}}\hat{\bar{D}}_{k_{1}}\hat{\bar{D}}_{k_{3}}

(111)

\displaystyle=\hat{D}_{k_{2}}\hat{D}_{k_{4}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s_{1}s_{2}ds_{1}ds_{2}}{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}(k_{12}+k_{34}+s_{1}+s_{2})(k_{12}+s_{1}+k)(k_{34}+s_{2}+k)}\hat{\bar{D}}_{k_{1}}\hat{\bar{D}}_{k_{3}}~,

where we have introduced Schwinger parameters
s_{i}
to regulate the IR divergences and express the
z
-integrals in a form that was already evaluated in section
3
. The integral can be performed using the hard-cutoff prescription by cutting off the
s-
integral at
s_{0}\to\infty
, with
s_{0}\sim z_{*}^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{s_{1}s_{2}ds_{1}ds_{2}}{(E+s_{1}+s_{2})(E_{L}+s_{1})(E_{R}+k)}\\
&=s_{0}\log 4-E-\frac{\pi^{2}}{12}(E_{L}-E_{R})\\
&+\frac{1}{2(E-E_{L}-E_{R})}\Bigg[12E_{L}(E_{L}-E)\mbox{Li}_{2}\frac{E_{L}-E}{E_{L}}+12E_{R}(E-E_{R})\mbox{Li}_{2}\frac{E_{R}}{E-E_{R}}\\
&\quad-2E_{L}E_{R}\log\frac{E_{L}}{s_{0}}\log\frac{s_{0}}{E_{R}}-2E(E_{L}+E_{R}+E)\log\frac{E}{s_{0}}\\
&\quad+E_{L}(E-E_{L})\log\frac{E_{L}}{s_{0}}\log\frac{(E-E_{L})}{E_{L}}+E_{R}(E-E_{R})\frac{E-E_{R}}{s_{0}}\log\frac{(E-E_{R})s_{0}}{E_{R}^{2}}\Bigg]\end{split}

(112)

where
E_{L}=k_{12}+k
,
E_{R}=k_{34}+k
,
E=k_{12}+k_{34}
.

Differential operators similar to the ones in equation (
108
) can be defined for other integer
m
using the following identity
15
15

15

Equation (
113
) is true for odd integers
n
. For other values such a recursion is generically not useful as it would involve an infinite number of derivatives. Alternatively one could use the spectral representation to obtain a correlator in Mellin space
[
90
,
91
,
92
,
23
,
24
]
. However, it is not straightforward to further perform the integral in the Mellin variable to obtain the result in momentum space.
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}K_{\frac{n}{2}}(kz)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\left(-\frac{1}{z}\right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\frac{k^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}{\sqrt{kz}}\left(\frac{1}{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\right)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\left(\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}\right)~,\end{split}

(113)

which gives us

\displaystyle\begin{split}&K_{m+\frac{1}{2}}(kz)+i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}K_{m-\frac{1}{2}}(kz)\\
&=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}}\frac{(-1)^{m-1}}{z^{m}}\frac{k^{m}}{\sqrt{kz}}\left\{\left(\frac{1}{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\right)^{m}-iz\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}\Big(\frac{1}{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\Big)^{m-1}\right\}\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}~.\end{split}

(114)

The second term above can be written in the form below in order to obtain the differential operator for any integer
m
. We provide the first few below,

\displaystyle z\left(\frac{1}{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\right)^{1}\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial k^{2}}+\frac{1}{k^{1}}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}-\frac{1}{k^{2}}\right)\frac{e^{-kz}}{k},

\displaystyle z\left(\frac{1}{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\right)^{2}\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{1}{k}\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial k^{3}}-\frac{3}{k^{3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}+\frac{3}{k^{4}}\right)\frac{e^{-kz}}{k},

(115)

\displaystyle z\left(\frac{1}{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\right)^{3}\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{1}{k^{2}}\frac{\partial^{4}}{\partial k^{4}}-\frac{2}{k^{3}}\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial k^{3}}-\frac{3}{k^{4}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial k^{2}}+\frac{15}{k^{5}}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}-\frac{15}{k^{6}}\right)\frac{e^{-kz}}{k},

\displaystyle z\left(\frac{1}{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\right)^{4}\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{1}{k^{3}}\frac{\partial^{5}}{\partial k^{5}}-\frac{5}{k^{4}}\frac{\partial^{4}}{\partial k^{4}}+\frac{5}{k^{5}}\frac{\partial^{3}}{\partial k^{3}}+\frac{30}{k^{6}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial k^{2}}-\frac{105}{k^{7}}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}+\frac{105}{k^{8}}\right)\frac{e^{-kz}}{k}~.

6.2
Internal Legs

It is also possible to obtain a set of recursion relations for the exchange of massive fermions. The massive Bulk-Bulk propagator is given as
[
53
]
16
16

16

We follow the conventions of
m\geq 0
and therefore this propagator satisfies the same boundary conditions as the massless case, as given in appendix
A
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{(m)}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})&=k\Bigg[\Theta(z_{1}-z_{2})\phi_{(m)}^{(K)}(z_{1},\vec{k})P_{-}\bar{\phi}_{(m)}^{(I)}(z_{2},-\vec{k})\\
&\qquad-\Theta(z_{2}-z_{1})\phi_{(m)}^{(I)}(z_{1},\vec{k})P_{-}\bar{\phi}_{(m)}^{(K)}(z_{2},-\vec{k})\Bigg]~,\end{split}

(116)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi_{(m)}^{(K)}(z,\vec{k})&=z^{\frac{d+1}{2}}\Bigg[K_{m+\frac{1}{2}}(kz)-i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}K_{m-\frac{1}{2}}(kz)\Bigg],\quad\bar{\phi}_{(m)}^{(K)}(z,\vec{k})=i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\phi^{(K)}(z,-\vec{k}),\\
\phi_{(m)}^{(I)}(z,\vec{k})&=z^{\frac{d+1}{2}}\Bigg[I_{m+\frac{1}{2}}(kz)+i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}I_{m-\frac{1}{2}}(kz)\Bigg],\quad\bar{\phi}_{(m)}^{(I)}(z,\vec{k})=-i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\phi^{(I)}(z,-\vec{k}).\end{split}

For the massless case, the propagator
\mathcal{S}^{(m=0)}
reduces to equation (
11
). Let us consider the case of
m=1
for which we get the following bulk-bulk propagator

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{(m=1)}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})

(117)

\displaystyle=k(z_{1}z_{2})^{2}\Bigg[\Theta(z_{1}-z_{2})\Big\{K_{3/2}(kz_{1})-i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}K_{1/2}(kz_{1})\Big\}P_{-}\Big\{i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}I_{3/2}(kz_{2})-I_{1/2}(kz_{2})\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+\Theta(z_{2}-z_{1})\Big\{I_{3/2}(kz_{1})+i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}I_{1/2}(kz_{1})\Big\}P_{-}\Big\{i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}K_{3/2}(kz_{2})+K_{1/2}(kz_{2})\Big\}\Bigg]~.

To derive a recursion relation for the massive case it is convenient to define the following quantities

\displaystyle\begin{split}F_{a,b}(z_{1},z_{2},k)&=(z_{1}z_{2})^{3/2}K_{a}(kz_{1})I_{b}(kz_{2}),\\
H(z_{1},z_{2},k)&=(z_{1}z_{2})^{1/2}K_{\frac{1}{2}}(kz_{1})I_{\frac{1}{2}}(kz_{2}).\end{split}

(118)

It is easily checked that these satisfy the following relations
17
17

17

A similar set of relations hold for
m\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\Delta}\mathcal{F}^{m-1}_{m+\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}&=((2m-1)(z_{1}+z_{2})-z_{1}z_{2}\hat{\Delta})\mathcal{F}^{m-2}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}},\\
\hat{\Delta}\mathcal{F}^{m-1}_{m+\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}&=((2m-1)(z_{1}+z_{2})-z_{1}z_{2}\hat{\Delta})\mathcal{F}^{m-2}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{3}{2}}+\frac{1}{k}(2m-1-z_{1}\hat{\Delta})\mathcal{F}^{m-2}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}},\\
\hat{\Delta}\mathcal{F}^{m-1}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}&=((2m-1)(z_{1}+z_{2})-z_{1}z_{2}\hat{\Delta})\mathcal{F}^{m-2}_{m-\frac{3}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}-\frac{1}{k}(2m-1-z_{2}\hat{\Delta})\mathcal{F}^{m-2}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}~,\end{split}

(119)

where
\mathcal{F}^{n}_{a,b}=(z_{1}z_{2})^{n+\frac{3}{2}}K_{a}(kz_{1})I_{b}(kz_{2})
. These relations hold for
\forall\ m
but are only useful to us for integer
m
. For
m=1
they reduce to (
120
).

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\Delta}H(z_{1},z_{2},k)&=e^{-kz_{1}}e^{-kz_{2}},\\
\hat{\Delta}F_{\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}}(z_{1},z_{2},k)&=(z_{1}+z_{2})H-(z_{1}z_{2})\hat{\Delta}H,\\
\hat{\Delta}F_{\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2}}(z_{1},z_{2},k)&=\Big(z_{1}+z_{2}+\frac{1}{k}\Big)H+\frac{z_{2}}{k}(1+kz_{1})\hat{\Delta}H,\\
\hat{\Delta}F_{\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}}(z_{1},z_{2},k)&=\Big(z_{1}+z_{2}-\frac{1}{k}\Big)H+\frac{z_{2}}{k}(1-kz_{1})\hat{\Delta}H~.\end{split}

(120)

where
\hat{\Delta}=\partial_{z_{1}}+\partial_{z_{2}}
. We also define the radial-ordered combination of
F
’s that enter the propagator,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{F}^{(\pm)}_{a,b}(z_{1},z_{2},k)=\Theta(z_{1}-z_{2})F_{a,b}(z_{1},z_{2},k)\pm\Theta(z_{2}-z_{1})F_{a,b}(z_{2},z_{1},k)~.\end{split}

(121)

In terms of these, the propagator (
117
) becomes
18
18

18

This equation can be generalized to all
m
via,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{(m)}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})

\displaystyle=k(z_{1}z_{2})^{1/2}\Bigg[\bar{F}_{m+\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}P_{-}\left(i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\right)+\bar{F}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}\left(i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\right)P_{-}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}^{(-)}\gamma^{z}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{m+\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{m+\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}^{(-)}\gamma^{z}\Bigg]

(122)

\displaystyle=k(z_{1}z_{2})^{1/2}\Bigg[\Big\{\bar{F}_{m+\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}+\bar{F}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}\Big\}\frac{i}{2}\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}+\Big\{\bar{F}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}-\bar{F}_{m+\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}\Big\}\frac{i}{2}\gamma^{z}\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{m+\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}\Big\}+\Big\{\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{m-\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{1}{2}}^{(-)}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{m+\frac{1}{2},m-\frac{1}{2}}^{(-)}\Big\}\gamma^{z}\Bigg]~.

For
m=1
this reduces to (
125
).

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{S}^{(m=1)}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})=k(z_{1}z_{2})^{1/2}&\Bigg[\bar{F}_{\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}}^{(+)}P_{-}\left(i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\right)+\bar{F}_{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}\left(i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\right)P_{-}\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}}^{(+)}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}}^{(-)}\gamma^{z}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{F}_{\frac{3}{2},\frac{1}{2}}^{(-)}\gamma^{z}\Bigg]~,\end{split}

(123)

where we suppress the
(z_{1},z_{2},k)
dependence of
\bar{F}
’s for brevity. Using this we can evaluate the Witten diagram with a massive fermion exchange. As a concrete example, we consider the diagram for the s-channel contribution to the correlator

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{I}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{2}e^{-x_{1}z_{1}}e^{-x_{2}z_{2}}&\Bigg[\Big\{F_{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}-F_{\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}}^{(+)}\Big\}i\gamma^{z}\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}+\Big\{F_{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}}^{(+)}+F_{\frac{3}{2},\frac{3}{2}}^{(+)}\Big\}i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\\

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.10887pt}{4.83542pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 4.3 6.69)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

.
For
m>1
, we would obtain a similar expression by using equations (
119
) and (
122
) with the same differential operators now acting on graphs with exchanged scalars of dimension
\Delta=m+1
. This shows how the massive fermions can be derived from the scalar graphs we saw in section
3
and a new antisymmetric graph (
127
), by the action of simple differential operators in a basis of independent gamma matrices. The differential operators can be thought of as the Weight shifting operators for massive fermions at the tree level. The recursion relations can also be generalized to higher point functions by following the same procedure as the massless case given in section
3
. In appendix
C
we describe how these recursion relations enable us to compute diagrams with massive internal legs at 1-loop.

6.3
IR Divergences

In the previous section, we encountered divergent integrals for massive external fermions. This is a feature of Witten diagrams which contain fields and interactions that are not conformally coupled. In these cases, upon performing the
z-
integral (or the integral over the Schwinger parameters
s
in equation (
111
)) we end up with divergences from the boundary
z=0
and these are known as IR divergences. These are studied very systematically for scalar fields in
[
93
,
32
]
. We summarize the basic idea behind the computations from the bulk side in a slightly different language by analyzing the divergences of fermionic correlators. For fields with any generic mass, the maximal IR divergence in the 3-pt function
\braket{\bar{\psi}\psi\phi}
in Yukawa theory receives a contribution from the following bulk-integral,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}dzz^{3/2}K_{m_{f}+\frac{1}{2}}(k_{1}z)K_{m_{f}+\frac{1}{2}}(k_{2}z)K_{\nu_{s}}(k_{3}z)~,\end{split}

(128)

where
\nu_{s}=\sqrt{\frac{9}{4}+m_{s}^{2}}
where
m_{f},m_{s}
are the masses of the fermion and the scalar respectively. This integral is IR divergent for specific values of
m_{f},\nu_{s}
and there are several prescriptions for regulating such divergences, including the hard-cutoff
[
94
,
95
,
96
]
and also using dimensional regularization
[
93
]
. In the following discussion, we shall work with dimensional regularization
21
21

21

In the language of
[
32
]
this is equivalent to setting
v=0
.
, which results in the following integral

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}dzz^{3/2}z^{\epsilon}K_{m_{f}+\frac{1}{2}}(k_{1}z)K_{m_{f}+\frac{1}{2}}(k_{2}z)K_{\nu_{s}}(k_{3}z)~,\end{split}

(129)

where we have introduced the regulator
\epsilon
. The general result of this integral can be expressed in terms of an Appell
F_{4}
function
[
97
,
93
]
. However, we find it convenient to analyze the singularities of the integral directly, without resorting to such a representation. The divergences in this integral can be classified into the following cases depending on the masses of the fields:

1.

m_{f}+\frac{1}{2}\neq Z
and
\nu_{s}\neq Z

For generic values of
m_{f},\nu_{s}
, the behavior of the integral near
z\to 0
is given as
22
22

22

These integrals can be written in terms of the Gamma function,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\frac{z^{\epsilon}}{z^{n}}e^{-z}=\Gamma(1-n+\epsilon)~.\end{split}

(130)

Since the
\Gamma
function has poles only at negative integers, such integrals are divergent as
\frac{1}{\epsilon}
only for
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\frac{z^{\epsilon}}{z^{a}}e^{-z}\sim\mbox{finite}~,\end{split}

(131)

where
a\neq\mathbb{Z}
, which gives is a finite contribution. For the special cases when
2m_{f}+\nu_{s}-\frac{1}{2}=\mathbb{Z}^{+}
(where
\mathbb{Z}^{+}
refers to positive integers greater than 0) we get divergences of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\frac{z^{\epsilon}}{z^{n}}f(z)&\sim\frac{1}{\epsilon}~,\end{split}

(132)

where
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

2.

m_{f}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
\nu_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}

In this case we only end up with integrals of the kind

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\frac{z^{\epsilon}}{z^{n}}f(z)&\sim\frac{1}{\epsilon}~.\end{split}

(133)

3.

m_{f}+\frac{1}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
\nu_{s}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}

In this case we encounter Bessel functions with integer arguments. Near
z\to 0
this has
\log
’s in the expansion around
z\to 0
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}K_{n}(z)\sim\frac{1}{z^{n}}+\cdots+z^{n-1}+z^{n}\log(z)+\cdots\end{split}

(134)

where
\log(z)
also appear in higher order terms but one never gets
\log^{2}(z)
or higher powers.

For
\nu_{s}-2m_{f}\geq\frac{7}{2}
we get integrals of the form
23
23

23

These integrals can be written in terms of a product of the Gamma function and PolyGamma function. For example,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\frac{z^{\epsilon}}{z^{n}}e^{-z}\log(z)=\Gamma(1-n+\epsilon)\psi^{(0)}(-n+\epsilon+1)\end{split}

(135)

where the PolyGamma function is defined as
\psi^{(n)}(x)=\frac{d^{n}}{dx^{n}}\frac{\Gamma^{\prime}(x)}{\Gamma(x)}
. As these are derivatives of the Gamma function, they also have poles only at negative integers. Integrals with more number of
\log(z)
insertions give a product such PolyGamma functions.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\frac{z^{\epsilon}}{z^{n}}\log(z)\log(z)e^{-z}&\sim\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}~,\end{split}

(136)

and for the case
\nu_{s}-2m_{f}<\frac{7}{2}
we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\frac{z^{\epsilon}}{z^{n}}\log(z)e^{-z}&\sim\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}~.\end{split}

(137)

We summarize the conditions for divergences in the table below

\nu_{s}

m_{f}

Max(Divergence)

\neq\mathbb{Z}

\neq\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
,
2m_{f}+\nu_{s}-\frac{1}{2}=2\mathbb{Z}^{+}

\epsilon^{-1}

\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}

\mathbb{Z}^{+}

\epsilon^{-1}

\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}

\mathbb{Z}^{+}-\frac{1}{2}
,
m_{f}>\frac{\nu_{s}}{2}-\frac{7}{4}

\epsilon^{-2}

\mathbb{Z}^{+}+\frac{1}{2}

\mathbb{Z}^{+}-\frac{1}{2}
,
m_{f}\leq\frac{\nu_{s}}{2}-\frac{7}{4}

\epsilon^{-3}

Table 1:
Classification of the order of IR divergences from bulk integrals for 3-pt functions.

The order of divergence is sensitive to the choice of the regularization scheme, for other schemes (like the cut-off), the powers and the analytic structure of the divergence would be different. In the regularization scheme chosen above, the degree of divergences encountered for 3-pt functions of fermions coupled to scalars are similar to the divergences for 3-pt functions of self-interacting scalars in AdS
4
as studied in
[
93
]
24
24

24

Similar to the analysis performed in
[
93
]
in position space, the divergences in momentum space can be categorized as ultralocal or sub-local, depending on their dependence on the momenta. For the 3-pt function, the ultralocal divergences arise when one obtains an expression as a function of
\sum_{i=1}^{3}f(k_{i})
, whereas sublocal divergences arise from
\sum_{i=1}^{3}f(k_{i},k_{i+1})

[
94
]
. This is because upon taking the Fourier transform w.r.t
k_{i}
, these give delta functions in position space and hence only click at coincident points.
. Therefore, the counterterms analogous to the ones proposed in
[
93
]
are expected to also renormalize these correlators and it would be interesting to make this precise in the future. A similar analysis can be performed for higher dimensions resulting in the same set of divergences.

For higher point exchange graphs the maximal divergence arises from a product of such contact graphs. This is understood by the following intuitive picture: IR divergences in the integrals arise when
z\to 0
. Hence, for any graph, the maximal divergence arises by squishing the entire Witten diagram to the boundary at
z\to 0
, reducing it to a product of lower point contact diagrams. In general, the limit of taking both radial points in the bulk-bulk propagator close to the boundary makes the radial integrals converge better. Hence the analysis for the maximal divergence performed above is sufficient for all graphs with polynomial interactions. For derivative interactions, the degree of divergence could increase depending on the number of derivatives in the interaction term, but the maximal divergence for any exchange graph is still less than equal to the product of contact graphs.

7
Cosmological Correlator

Correlation functions in AdS are also useful for computing in-in correlators in dS and flat spacetime. This is usually done by relating the on-shell action in AdS to the ground state wave function in dS
[
98
]
via an analytical continuation of the form
[
52
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Psi_{dS}(\phi,\psi,\bar{\psi})=e^{-S^{AdS}_{on-shell}(\phi,\psi,\bar{\psi})}~,\end{split}

(138)

where
S^{AdS}_{on-shell}(\phi,\psi,\bar{\psi})
has the following expansion
25
25

25

In this section we use
a,b,c,d
to denote the Spinor indices.
,

\displaystyle S^{AdS}_{on-shell}(\phi,\psi,\bar{\psi})

\displaystyle=\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}W^{ab}_{2}[\bar{\psi},\psi]+\int\phi\phi W_{2}^{(\phi)}[\phi,\phi]+\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\phi W_{3}^{ab}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi]

(139)

\displaystyle+\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\phi\phi W_{4}^{ab}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi,\phi]+\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\bar{\psi}_{c}\psi_{d}W_{4}^{abcd}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\bar{\psi},\psi]+\cdots

where we suppress the momentum dependence in the integrands to avoid a clutter of notation.
W_{n}
’s are referred to as the
wave function coefficients
. Since these are evaluated via an analytic continuation of the on-shell action in EAdS, they are computed by Witten diagrams and therefore can be evaluated by using the tools in the previous sections. For IR divergent integrals the analytical continuation in (
138
) has to be performed carefully (see
[
99
]
for a recent discussion on IR divergences for scalar theories) and for this section, we shall only restrict to massless fermions coupled to conformally coupled scalars where there are no IR divergences
26
26

26

The issue of renormalizing IR divergences is more subtle in dS than for AdS and it would be interesting to understand the EFT for fermions in dS by following
[
100
]
.
. The Cosmological Correlator (or the In-In correlator) in the state
\Psi_{dS}
is defined as,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\Psi_{dS}|\mathcal{O}_{1}(\vec{k}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{n}(\vec{k}_{n})|\Psi_{dS}}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}\int D\bar{\psi}D\psi D\phi\big|\Psi_{dS}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi]\big|^{2}\mathcal{O}_{1}(\vec{k}_{1})\cdots\mathcal{O}_{n}(\vec{k}_{n}),\end{split}

(140)

where
\mathcal{O}_{1}(\vec{k}_{1}),\cdots,\mathcal{O}_{n}(\vec{k}_{n})
can be either fermionic fields or scalar fields at the conformal boundary and
\mathcal{N}
is the normalization factor for the path integral

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{N}=\int D\bar{\psi}D\psi D\phi\big|\Psi_{dS}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi]\big|^{2}~.\end{split}

(141)

This path integral can be directly performed using standard methods
[
52
]
and can also be performed using the Schwinger-Keldysh (In-In) approach
[
101
]
. For our purpose, we shall directly evaluate the path integral above. Using (
138
) and (
139
), the full ground state wave function
\Psi_{dS}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi]
can be expressed in terms of the wave-function coefficients as

\displaystyle\Psi_{dS}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi]

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}W^{ab}_{2}[\bar{\psi},\psi]-\int\phi\phi W_{2}^{(\phi)}[\phi,\phi]\Big)

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big(-\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\phi W_{3}^{ab}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi]\Big)

(142)

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big(-\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\phi\phi W_{4}^{ab}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi,\phi]-\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\bar{\psi}_{c}\psi_{d}W_{4}^{abcd}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\bar{\psi},\psi]\Big)\cdots

where we have explicitly displayed the spinor indices. Squaring the equation above we obtain an effective action for the cosmological correlator (
140
),

\displaystyle\big|\Psi_{dS}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi]\big|^{2}

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(-2\mbox{Re}\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}W^{ab}_{2}[\bar{\psi},\psi]-2\mbox{Re}\int\phi\phi W_{2}^{(\phi)}[\phi,\phi]\Big)

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big(-2\mbox{Re}\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\phi W_{3}^{ab}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi]\Big)

(143)

\displaystyle\times\exp\Big(-2\mbox{Re}\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\phi\phi W_{4}^{ab}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi,\phi]-2\mbox{Re}\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\bar{\psi}_{c}\psi_{d}W_{4}^{abcd}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\bar{\psi},\psi]\Big)\cdots

This can be viewed as an effective action for the path integral in (
140
) and can be used to compute any correlator of interest using perturbation theory. For concreteness, we evaluate the following 4-pt correlator at leading order in the coupling constant in
\frac{g}{z}\phi^{2}\bar{\psi}\psi
theory,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\Psi_{dS}|\phi(\vec{k}_{1})\phi(\vec{k}_{2})\phi(\vec{k}_{3})\phi(\vec{k}_{4})|\Psi_{dS}}&=\mathcal{N}\int D\psi D\bar{\psi}D\phi\phi(\vec{k}_{1})\phi(\vec{k}_{2})\phi(\vec{k}_{3})\phi(\vec{k}_{4})\big|\Psi_{dS}\big|^{2}.\end{split}

(144)

We also provide an appendix
D
illustrating the details of evaluating the path integral to obtain correlators with external fermions
\braket{\Psi_{dS}|\bar{\psi}_{i}(\vec{k}_{1})\phi(\vec{k}_{2})\psi_{j}(\vec{k}_{3})\phi(\vec{k}_{4})|\Psi_{dS}}
. The leading order contribution to the cosmological correlator (
144
) occurs at
O(g^{2})
and receives a contribution from the following wave function coefficients

\displaystyle\begin{split}W_{4}^{(1)ab}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi,\phi]&=\scalebox{0.75}{\hbox to122.55pt{\vbox to115.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 61.27498pt\lower-57.50551pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{52.94197pt}{28.78714pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 73.26 39.83)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}} }=g^{2}\times\mbox{equation \eqref{bubble-x1x2-ans}}.\end{split}

(145)

which results in the following contribution to the cosmological correlator at
O(g^{2})
for the s-channel

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\braket{\Psi_{dS}|\phi(\vec{k}_{1})\phi(\vec{k}_{2})\phi(\vec{k}_{3})\phi(\vec{k}_{4})|\Psi_{dS}}\\
&=\int d^{3}l\ \mbox{Re}W_{4}^{(1)ab}[\bar{\psi}(l_{1}),\psi(\vec{l}_{2}),\phi(\vec{k}_{1}),\phi(\vec{k}_{2})]\mbox{Re}W_{4}^{(1)ba}[\bar{\psi}(l_{2}),\psi(\vec{l}_{1}),\phi(\vec{k}_{3}),\phi(\vec{k}_{4})]\\
&\quad+2\mbox{Re}W_{4}^{(2)}[\phi(\vec{k}_{1}),\phi(\vec{k}_{2}),\phi(\vec{k}_{3}),\phi(\vec{k}_{4})]~.\end{split}

(146)

The two terms are explicitly given as
27
27

27

The real part of the product of
\gamma
-matrices is computed using

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mbox{Re}\big[(1+i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{l}_{1}}{l_{1}})(1+i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{l}_{2}}{l_{2}})\big]=1-\frac{\vec{l}_{1}\cdot\vec{l}_{2}}{l_{1}l_{2}}\end{split}

which uses the fact that the gamma matrices in the Euclidean signature are hermitian.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mbox{Re}W_{4}^{(1)ab}\mbox{Re}W_{4}^{(1)ba}&=\frac{2}{(l_{1}+l_{2}+k_{12})(l_{1}+l_{2}+k_{34})}\Bigg(1+\frac{\vec{l}_{1}\cdot\vec{l}_{2}}{l_{1}l_{2}}\Bigg)^{2},\\
\mbox{Re}W_{4}^{(2)}&=\Bigg[-\frac{1}{(l_{1}+l_{2}+k_{34})(k_{12}+k_{34})}-\frac{1}{(l_{1}+l_{2}+k_{12})(k_{12}+k_{34})}\\
&\qquad+\frac{1}{(l_{1}+l_{2}+k_{12})(l_{1}+l_{2}+k_{34})}\Bigg]\Bigg(1+\frac{\vec{l}_{1}\cdot\vec{l}_{2}}{l_{1}l_{2}}\Bigg)~,\end{split}

(147)

where
l_{1}=|\vec{l}|
and
l_{2}=|\vec{l}+\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}|
. As an immediate consistency check, we see that only the second term,
\mbox{Re}W_{4}^{(2)}
, contributes to the flat space limit, recovering the expected integrand for the bubble diagram in flat space. The integral over
\vec{l}
can be performed in the same manner as the wave function coefficient in section
5.1
. Upon evaluating the integral
28
28

28

For the interested readers, the full result is recorded in the following
Mathematica
notebook which can be found in
[
102
]
.
we find that the disconnected piece,
\mbox{Re}W_{4}^{(1)ab}\mbox{Re}W_{4}^{(1)ba}
, is a
\mbox{Li}_{2}
in the external momenta whereas the other term,
\mbox{Re}W_{4}^{(2)}
, gives a
\log
. This suggests that for spinning exchanges, in-in correlators can have a higher transcendentality than the corresponding wave function coefficient. We also discuss another example of a spinning correlator in appendix
D
. It would be interesting to compare the pole structures of the in-in correlator vs the wave function coefficients more generally for fermions. A similar analysis was carried out for scalars in
[
103
]
and it would be useful to explore this further using the shadow formalism
[
104
,
105
,
106
,
107
]
. This formalism suggests that in-in correlators of fields with conformal dimension
\Delta
are directly obtained by evaluating Witten diagrams for an effective action in EAdS
d+1
constructed out of their shadow fields (which have a conformal dimension
d-\Delta
). For scalars, this provides an easier way to evaluate loop integrals for cosmological correlators as shown in
[
20
,
18
]
and it would be interesting to see if a similar statement also holds for fermions.

Appendix A
Review of perturbation theory for scalars and fermions

In this appendix we review the derivation of propagators for scalars and fermions in Euclidean AdS in the Poincare patch.
The metric is given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}ds^{2}=\frac{dz^{2}+dx_{1}^{2}+\cdots+dx_{d}^{2}}{z^{2}}~,\end{split}

(148)

where
z
is the radial coordinate which ranges from
(0,\infty)
and
x_{i}
’s are the other spatial directions ranging from
(-\infty,\infty)
. The volume element is given as
\sqrt{g}=\frac{1}{z^{d+1}}
.

A.1
Scalars

Consider a scalar in AdS with the action given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}S=\int d^{d+1}x\sqrt{g}\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\varphi\partial^{\mu}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}m^{2}\varphi^{2}\right)~.\end{split}

(149)

The equation of motion of the scalar field is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{\sqrt{g}}\partial_{\mu}(\sqrt{g}\partial^{\mu}\varphi)=z^{d+1}\partial_{z}\left(\frac{1}{z^{d-1}}\partial_{z}\varphi\right)+z^{2}\partial_{i}^{2}\varphi=m^{2}\varphi~.\end{split}

(150)

The field
\phi
can be decomposed in terms of its Fourier modes,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varphi(z,\vec{x})=\int\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\phi(z,\vec{k})~.\end{split}

(151)

This leads to the following differential equation for
\phi(z,\vec{k})
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}z^{2}\partial_{z}^{2}\phi-(d-1)z\partial_{z}\phi-(k^{2}z^{2}+m^{2})\phi=0~,\end{split}

(152)

and its solution is given as,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi(z,\vec{k})=c_{1}z^{d/2}J_{\nu}(\sqrt{-k^{2}}z)+c_{2}z^{d/2}Y_{\nu}(\sqrt{-k^{2}}z)~,\end{split}

(153)

where
\nu=\frac{\sqrt{d^{2}+4m^{2}}}{2}
. For the massless case when
\nu=\frac{d}{2}
, expanding this about
z\to\infty
we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{z\to\infty}\phi(z,\vec{k})=ze^{kz}\frac{(-1)^{1/4}}{\sqrt{2\pi k}}\left(ic_{1}-c_{2}\right)+\mbox{regular}~.\end{split}

(154)

Thus choosing
c_{2}=ic_{1}
ensures that the field is normalizable as
z\to\infty
. A similar conclusion holds for any general mass of the scalar field. Therefore for any general mass, the field becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi(z,\vec{k})=c_{2}z^{d/2}\left\{iJ_{\nu}(\sqrt{-k^{2}}z)-Y_{\nu}(\sqrt{-k^{2}}z)\right\}.\end{split}

(155)

Absorbing the extra factors into the normalization and demanding that
k^{2}<0
for spacelike momenta, we obtain the usual solution in terms of the modified Bessel function of the second kind

\displaystyle\begin{split}\phi(z,\vec{k})=c_{2}z^{d/2}K_{\nu}(kz)~.\end{split}

(156)

The normalization constant can be fixed via the boundary value of the scalar field. This becomes the bulk-boundary Green function. The bulk-bulk Green function can be derived in multiple ways. The simplest way to derive this in Euclidean space is to solve the differential equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\big\{z^{2}\partial_{z}^{2}-(d-1)z\partial_{z}-(k^{2}z^{2}+m^{2})\big\}G(z,z^{\prime},\vec{k})=iz^{d+1}\delta(z-z^{\prime})~.\end{split}

(157)

This has to satisfy the boundary condition that it is normalizable as
z\to 0
and
z\to\infty
and is continuous across
z=z^{\prime}
. Along with demanding that the Green function is normalizable as
z\to 0
, for special values of mass, we also require it to fall off as
z^{\Delta}
as
z\to 0
, where
\Delta=\nu+\frac{d}{2}
. This completely fixes the Green function for all masses and we arrive at the standard expression for the Green function of a scalar field in AdS satisfying normalizable boundary condition (see section 2.1 of
[
99
]
for a very systematic derivation),

\displaystyle\begin{split}G(z,z^{\prime},\vec{k})=(zz^{\prime})^{d/2}\Big\{\Theta(z-z^{\prime})K_{\nu}(kz)I_{\nu}(kz^{\prime})+\Theta(z^{\prime}-z)K_{\nu}(kz^{\prime})I_{\nu}(kz)\Big\}~.\end{split}

(158)

For
d=3
and
\Delta=1,2
we recover the expressions given in section
2
.

A.2
Fermions

We
29
29

29

We are grateful to Shivam Sharma for several helpful discussions and also pointing out typos in the previous version of the equations.
review the derivation of the propagators for massless fermions in AdS
4
for simplicity, the general dimensional massive case is conceptually similar and is discussed in detail in
[
53
,
59
,
108
]
30
30

30

It is well known that the on-shell action for the free fermion is zero and it needs to be supplemented by a boundary term of the form
\bar{\psi}\psi

[
109
]
. For computing tree-level correlators for interacting theories this choice is equivalent to using the bulk-bulk and bulk-boundary propagators given below (see
[
110
,
56
,
111
]
for a discussion of the propagators used in computing out of time-ordered correlators). It would be interesting to investigate this more carefully for loop-level correlators.
.

The differential equation for the equation of motion of a minimally coupled massless free Dirac fermion in AdS
4
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\not{D}\psi_{AdS}=\big(z\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}-\frac{3}{2}\gamma^{z}\big)\psi_{AdS}=0~.\end{split}

(159)

We use the following ansatz to solve it

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{AdS}(z,\vec{x})=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}A(\vec{k})\chi_{AdS}z^{3/2}e^{-kz}e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}~,\end{split}

(160)

where
A(\vec{k})
is a
4\times 4
matrix and
\chi_{AdS}
denotes the boundary limit of the bulk spinor
31
31

31

This can be related with the lower dimensional boundary spinor via a rectangular matrix
[
56
]
.
. Using this ansatz we obtain the following for
\not{D}\psi_{AdS}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\not{D}\psi_{AdS}&=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\big(z\gamma^{z}\partial_{z}+iz\gamma^{i}k_{i}-\frac{3}{2}\gamma^{z}\big)(z^{3/2}A\chi_{AdS}e^{-kz})\\
&=-i\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}z^{5/2}e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\big(i\gamma^{z}\partial_{z}-\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}\big)A\chi_{AdS}e^{-kz}~,\end{split}

(161)

The boundary condition we impose is the standard Dirichlet boundary condition
[
53
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{z\to 0}P_{-}\psi_{AdS}(z,\vec{x})=\lim_{z\to 0}z^{3/2}\chi_{AdS}~,\end{split}

(162)

where the power of
z
indicates the scaling dimension and
P_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(1\pm\gamma^{z})
. This boundary condition sets two out of the four spinor components to zero at the boundary. From the ansatz given above (
160
), the most general solution of the equation of motion (
161
) is obtained via the following matrix
A(\vec{k})
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}A(\vec{k})=1+i\gamma^{z}\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}~.\end{split}

(163)

By imposing the boundary condition (
162
) we get the following constraint on the spinor
\chi_{AdS}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\gamma^{z}\chi_{AdS}=-\chi_{AdS}~.\end{split}

(164)

Hence the bulk-boundary propagator (
160
) can also be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}\psi_{AdS}(z,\vec{x})=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}z^{3/2}e^{-kz}e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\left(1+i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\right)\chi_{AdS}~,\end{split}

(165)

for
\gamma^{z}\chi_{AdS}=-\chi_{AdS}
. Similar relations also hold for
\bar{\psi}_{AdS}(z,x)
, for which we make the standard choice

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{\psi}_{AdS}(z,\vec{x})=\psi_{AdS}^{\dagger}(z,\vec{x})\gamma^{t}~,\end{split}

(166)

although any linear combination of
\gamma_{t},\gamma_{x},\gamma_{y}
would be equivalent since we are in EAdS. The boundary condition for
\bar{\psi}_{AdS}
is,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{z\to 0}\bar{\psi}_{AdS}P_{-}=\lim_{z\to 0}z^{3/2}\bar{\chi}_{AdS}~,\end{split}

(167)

where
\bar{\chi}_{AdS}=\chi_{AdS}^{\dagger}\gamma^{t}
and it satisfies
\bar{\chi}_{AdS}=\bar{\chi}_{AdS}\gamma^{z}
. It can be checked that (
166
) is consistent with the equation of motion for
\bar{\psi}_{AdS}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{\psi}_{AdS}\overset{\leftarrow}{\not{D}}=0~,\end{split}

(168)

where
\overset{\leftarrow}{\not{D}}=\gamma^{z}\overset{\leftarrow}{\partial}_{z}+\gamma_{i}\overset{\leftarrow}{\partial}_{i}-\frac{3}{2}\gamma^{z}
, which after acting on the spinor effectively becomes
-\gamma^{z}k-i\gamma^{i}k_{i}-\frac{3}{2}\gamma^{z}
. Hence the bulk-boundary propagator
\bar{\psi}_{AdS}
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{\psi}_{AdS}(z,\vec{x})=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}z^{3/2}e^{-kz}e^{-i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\bar{\chi}_{AdS}\left(1+i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\right)~.\end{split}

(169)

The bulk-bulk propagator solves the following equation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\not{D}S_{AdS}(z,z^{\prime},\vec{k})=(z\gamma^{z}\partial_{z}+iz\gamma^{i}k_{i}-\frac{3}{2}\gamma^{z})S_{AdS}(z,z^{\prime},\vec{k})=\delta(z,z^{\prime})~,\end{split}

(170)

which can be solved in the same way as the scalar case. The boundary condition that fixes the propagator is obtained from (
162
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{z\to 0}P_{-}S_{AdS}(z,z^{\prime},\vec{k})=z^{3/2}(\cdots)~,\end{split}

(171)

where
\cdots
denote the terms regular in
z
. The final solution is given as

\displaystyle S_{AdS}(z,z^{\prime},\vec{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{(zz^{\prime})^{3/2}}{2}\Bigg[\Theta(z-z^{\prime})\left(\gamma^{z}-i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\right)e^{-k(z-z^{\prime})}-\Theta(z^{\prime}-z)\left(\gamma^{z}+i\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\right)e^{-k(z^{\prime}-z)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\left(1+i\gamma^{z}\frac{\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}}{k}\right)e^{-k(z+z^{\prime})}\Bigg]~.

(172)

As a consequence we also have
\lim_{z^{\prime}\to 0}S_{AdS}(z,z^{\prime},\vec{k})P_{+}=0
. In section
6
we also provide the explicit expression for the propagators of massive fermions in AdS
4
.

A.3
Spectral Representation

It is often convenient to use an alternate representation of the bulk-bulk propagator for computing integrals for diverse masses or regulating divergences. One such representation is known as the
spectral representation
. This can be derived by expressing the fermionic bulk-bulk propagator as a derivative of the scalar propagator as given in equation (
20
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}S(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})=-(\gamma^{z}\partial_{z_{1}}+i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k})\mathcal{G}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})\end{split}

where
\mathcal{G}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})=P_{+}G_{D}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})+P_{-}G_{N}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})
with their spectral representations given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{D}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})&=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\omega}{\omega^{2}+k^{2}}\sin(\omega z_{1})\sin(\omega z_{2}),\\
G_{N}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})&=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\omega}{\omega^{2}+k^{2}}\cos(\omega z_{1})\cos(\omega z_{2}).\end{split}

(173)

Using these, we have the following spectral representation for the fermionic bulk-bulk propagator in flat space,

\displaystyle\begin{split}S(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})&=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\omega}{\omega^{2}+k^{2}}\Bigg[\omega\Big\{\sin\big(\omega(z_{1}+z_{2})\big)-\gamma^{z}\sin\big(\omega(z_{1}-z_{2})\big)\Big\}\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad+i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}\Big\{\cos\big(\omega(z_{1}-z_{2})\big)-\gamma^{z}\cos\big(\omega(z_{1}+z_{2})\big)\Big\}\Bigg]\end{split}

(174)

This representation naturally generalizes to a form that regularizes both IR and UV divergences in AdS,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&S^{AdS}_{reg}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})\\
&=-\frac{(z_{1}z_{2})^{3/2+\kappa}}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\omega}{(\omega^{2}+k^{2})^{1+\kappa}}\Bigg[\omega\Big\{\sin\big(\omega(z_{1}+z_{2})\big)-\gamma^{z}\sin\big(\omega(z_{1}-z_{2})\big)\Big\}\\
&\hskip 142.26378pt+i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}\Big\{\cos\big(\omega(z_{1}-z_{2})\big)-\gamma^{z}\cos\big(\omega(z_{1}+z_{2})\big)\Big\}\Bigg]\end{split}

(175)

where
\kappa
is the regulator. This regularization technique is known as
analytic regularization
and was developed in
[
18
]
for scalars in momentum space
32
32

32

The spectral representation also allows one to derive the “cut propagator’ which enables the study of discontinuities of correlators w.r.t external momenta. These are analogous to Cutting rules
[
112
]
for the flat space S-matrix where the propagator goes on-shell. For tree-level correlators, it is shown
[
113
,
55
]
how the discontinuities of AdS correlators and dS wave functions are constrained via the spectral propagator going on-shell. It is also possible to extend this for fermionic correlators via picking the pole at
\omega=ik
in equation (
174
) to obtain the fermionic “cut propagator”

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{cut}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})=\frac{1}{2}&\Bigg[k\Big\{\sin\big(k(z_{1}+z_{2})\big)-\gamma^{z}\sin\big(k(z_{1}-z_{2})\big)\Big\}\\
&\qquad+i\vec{\gamma}\cdot\vec{k}\Big\{\cos\big(k(z_{1}-z_{2})\big)-\gamma^{z}\cos\big(k(z_{1}+z_{2})\big)\Big\}\Bigg]\end{split}

(176)

It would be interesting to use the cut propagators to constrain the behavior of loop-level correlators which we leave for future work.

. This representation enables one to perform integrals for the cosmological correlator due to a hidden structure of rewriting them as flat space integrals
[
18
]
. However, is not trivial to implement the same algorithm AdS correlators and we hope to explore these integrals in future work.

4.3

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{21.26299pt}{-2.66666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 29.42 -3.69)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{2}dz_{3}e^{-k_{s}z_{2}}F_{L}(z_{1})S(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})\gamma_{i}\epsilon^{i}_{s}S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{k}+\vec{k}_{s})F_{R}(z_{3})\end{split}

(177)

where the gray blobs are denoted by
F_{L}(z_{1})
and
F_{R}(z_{3})
and
\epsilon_{s}^{i}\equiv\epsilon^{i}(\vec{k}_{s})
. Since
F_{L}(z_{1})
and
F_{R}(z_{3})
are arbitrary functions of momenta, the soft limit derived for this diagram is equally applicable for loop diagrams where the soft leg is attached to an edge which carries loop momenta. In the soft limit, by momentum conservation at the vertex in the middle, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{\vec{k}_{s}\to 0}Y=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{2}dz_{3}F_{L}(z_{1})S(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})\gamma_{i}\epsilon^{i}_{s}S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{k})F_{R}(z_{3})~.\end{split}

(178)

After some heavy algebra, we obtain a simple result for the
z_{2}
-integral,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{2}S(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{k})\gamma_{i}S(z_{2},z_{3},\vec{k})\\
&=2i\frac{k^{j}}{k}\Bigg\{2\delta_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})+\frac{\Sigma_{ij}}{k}\big[S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})-S(z_{1},z_{3},-\vec{k})\big]\Bigg\}~.\end{split}

(179)

Which gives the following expression for (
178
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{\vec{k}_{s}\to 0}Y&=4i\frac{k^{j}\epsilon_{s}^{i}}{k}\delta_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{3}F_{L}(z_{1})S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})F_{R}(z_{3})\\
&\qquad+2i\frac{k^{j}\epsilon_{s}^{i}}{k^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{3}F_{L}(z_{1})\Sigma^{ij}\big[S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})-S(z_{1},z_{3},-\vec{k})\big]F_{R}(z_{3})~.\end{split}

(180)

By using

\displaystyle\begin{split}S(z_{1},z_{3},-\vec{k})=\gamma^{z}S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})\gamma^{z},\end{split}

(181)

the term proportional to
\Sigma^{ij}
can also be written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&2i\frac{k_{j}}{k^{2}}\Sigma^{ij}\Big[S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})-\gamma^{z}S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})\gamma^{z}\Big]=2i\frac{k_{j}}{k^{2}}\Sigma^{ij}\Big[P_{-}S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})P_{+}+P_{+}S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})P_{-}\Big]~.\end{split}

(182)

Hence we obtain the soft limit for the class of diagrams where the photon is attached to an internal leg,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{\vec{k}_{s}\to 0}Y&=4i\frac{k^{j}\epsilon_{s}^{i}}{k}\delta_{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{3}F_{L}(z_{1})S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})F_{R}(z_{3})\\
&\quad+2i\frac{k^{j}\epsilon_{s}^{i}}{k^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{3}F_{L}(z_{1})\Sigma^{ij}P_{-}S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})P_{+}F_{R}(z_{3})\\
&\quad+2i\frac{k^{j}\epsilon_{s}^{i}}{k^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{3}F_{L}(z_{1})\Sigma^{ij}P_{+}S(z_{1},z_{3},\vec{k})P_{-}F_{R}(z_{3})~.\end{split}

(183)

The terms proportional to
\Sigma^{ij}
would be absent for scalars coupled to photons and we would only have the term with the derivative
\frac{\partial}{\partial k}
. We summarize the relation diagrammatically below

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{\vec{k}_{s}\to 0}\hbox to115.01pt{\vbox to130.6pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 57.50551pt\lower-57.50551pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{split}

(184)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{T}_{r_{1}r_{2}}=2ik^{j}\epsilon_{s}^{i}\left[2\frac{\delta_{ij}}{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial k}+\frac{1}{k^{2}}\big\{(\Sigma_{ij}P_{-})_{r_{1}r_{2}}+(P_{-}\Sigma_{ij})_{r_{2}r_{1}}\big\}\right]~.\end{split}

(185)

Hence we see that the soft limit of this class of diagrams does not factorize into the original lower point correlators but does so into diagrams with a modified bulk-bulk propagator. Unlike the case of the soft limit for the diagrams discussed in section
4.3
, the individual terms in (
183
) are not trivially discernible from each other as they have the same pole structure. However, for this specific theory, they can still be discerned in a unique way from the rest of the terms due to the order of poles in the exchange momenta and therefore provide useful consistency checks.

Appendix C
Massive Loop Integrals

In this appendix, we demonstrate how the recursion relations described for the massive fields in section
6
work at 1-loop. For simplicity, we consider the examples of scalar fields in the internal legs, but a similar strategy also works for fermions. Consider the two-site bubble graph with a scalar of mass
m=0
in one internal leg of the loop and a scalar of mass
m^{2}=-2
in another internal leg of the loop. To illustrate the structure of the integral we consider an interaction term
z\phi^{3}\zeta
where
\phi
is conformally coupled and
\zeta
is massless. The bubble diagram which appears in the 4-pt function
\braket{\phi(\vec{k}_{1})\phi(\vec{k}_{2})\phi(\vec{k}_{3})\phi(\vec{k}_{4})}
is

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=\int_{0}^{\infty}dz_{1}dz_{2}e^{-x_{1}z_{1}}e^{-x_{2}z_{2}}G_{\phi}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{y}_{1})G_{\zeta}(z_{2},z_{1},\vec{y}_{2})\end{split}

(186)

where
x_{1}=k_{12},\ x_{2}=k_{34}
,
y_{1}=|\vec{l}|,y_{2}=|\vec{l}+\vec{k}_{1}+\vec{k}_{2}|
. The field
\zeta
is represented by red lines and the field
\phi
, by black lines. The bulk-bulk propagators for
\phi
and
\zeta
are

\displaystyle\begin{split}G_{\phi}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{y}_{1})&=(z_{1}z_{2})^{1/2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\omega}{\omega^{2}+y_{1}^{2}}J_{1/2}(\omega z_{1})J_{1/2}(\omega z_{2})\\
G_{\zeta}(z_{1},z_{2},\vec{y}_{2})&=(z_{1}z_{2})^{3/2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d\omega}{\omega^{2}+y_{2}^{2}}J_{3/2}(\omega z_{1})J_{3/2}(\omega z_{2})~.\end{split}

(187)

. The remaining term in (

which results in simple graphs with conformally coupled exchanges. This shows that the intermediate steps indeed simplify by using the tree theorem and also shows how the tree theorem is also useful for diagrams with non-conformally coupled internal legs. By evaluating the integral in
\vec{l}
we obtain a Li
2
in the external momenta which is similar to the conformally coupled case. It would be interesting to generalize this approach to studying the transcendentality of functions obtained for generic masses including cases when we have IR divergences.

Appendix D
In-In Correlators with External Fermions

In this appendix, we provide further details on computing the path integral for the cosmological correlator with external fermions. To be concrete, we evaluate the following correlator in Yukawa theory,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\Psi_{dS}|\bar{\psi}_{i}(\vec{k}_{1})\phi(\vec{k}_{2})\psi_{j}(\vec{k}_{3})\phi(\vec{k}_{4})|\Psi_{dS}}=\int D\phi D\psi D\bar{\psi}\bar{\psi}_{i}(\vec{k}_{1})\phi(\vec{k}_{2})\psi_{j}(\vec{k}_{3})\phi(\vec{k}_{4})|\Psi_{dS}|^{2}\end{split}

(194)

Tree-Level:
\bm{O(\lambda^{2})}

The 2-pt functions are normalized as,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\bar{\psi}_{a}(\vec{q})\psi_{b}(\vec{q}^{\prime})}\equiv\frac{1}{\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{2}(q,q^{\prime})}\delta(\vec{q}-\vec{q}^{\prime})&=\frac{(\gamma\cdot q)^{ab}}{q}\delta(\vec{q}-\vec{q}^{\prime}),\\
\braket{\phi(\vec{q})\phi(\vec{q}^{\prime})}\equiv\frac{1}{\mbox{Re}W^{(\phi)}_{2}(q,q^{\prime})}\delta(\vec{q}-\vec{q}^{\prime})&=q\delta(\vec{q}-\vec{q}^{\prime}).\end{split}

(195)

From the Witten diagram computation of the 3-pt function we get,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{3}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})=\frac{\bar{u}^{a}(q_{1})u^{b}(q_{2})}{q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3}}\delta(\vec{q}_{1}+\vec{q}_{2}+\vec{q}_{3})\end{split}

(196)

The 3-pt function at tree level is given as,

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})}

\displaystyle=\int D\bar{\psi}D\psi D\phi\exp\Big(-\mbox{Re}\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}W^{ab}_{2}[\bar{\psi},\psi]-\mbox{Re}\int\phi\phi W_{2}^{(\phi)}[\phi,\phi]\Big)

\displaystyle\quad\times\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\Big(1-\mbox{Re}\int\bar{\psi}_{a}\psi_{b}\phi W_{3}^{ab}[\bar{\psi},\psi,\phi]\Big)

(197)

Plugging everything explicitly, we obtain the following

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})}

\displaystyle=\int dq_{1}dq_{2}dq_{3}\frac{\delta(q_{3}-p_{3})}{\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{\phi}(q_{3},p_{3})}\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{3}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\frac{\delta(q_{1}-p_{2})}{q_{1}}(\gamma\cdot q_{1})^{ja}\frac{\delta(q_{2}-p_{1})}{q_{2}}(\gamma\cdot q_{2})^{ib}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{\phi}(p_{3},p_{3})}\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{3}(p_{2},p_{1},p_{3})\frac{1}{p_{1}}(\gamma\cdot p_{2})^{ja}\frac{1}{p_{2}}(\gamma\cdot p_{1})^{ib}

(198)

For the 4-pt function, we divide the contribution into two terms, one coming from the product of
W_{3}
’s and the other coming from
W_{4}
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}\braket{\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})}=\braket{\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\bar{\psi}^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})}^{(1)}+\braket{\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})}^{(2)}\end{split}

(199)

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\braket{\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\bar{\psi}^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})}^{(1)}=\int dq_{1}\cdots dq_{6}\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{3}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\mbox{Re}W^{cd}_{3}(q_{4},q_{5},q_{6})\\
&\qquad\times\int D\phi D\bar{\psi}D\psi\bar{\psi}_{a}(q_{1})\psi_{b}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\bar{\psi}_{c}(q_{4})\psi_{d}(q_{5})\phi(q_{6})\times\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})e^{-S_{quad}}\end{split}

(200)

and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\braket{\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\bar{\psi}^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})}^{(2)}=\int dq_{1}\cdots dq_{4}\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{4}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4})\\
&\qquad\times\int D\phi D\bar{\psi}D\psi\bar{\psi}_{a}(q_{1})\psi_{b}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\phi(q_{4})\times\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})e^{-S_{quad}}\end{split}

(201)

The correlators also receive contributions from disconnected diagrams but we only list the connected contributions below. The first term is given as

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\bar{\psi}^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})}^{(1)}=\int dq_{1}\cdots dq_{6}\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{3}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\mbox{Re}W^{cd}_{3}(q_{4},q_{5},q_{6})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int D\phi D\bar{\psi}D\psi\bar{\psi}_{a}(q_{1})\psi_{b}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\bar{\psi}_{c}(q_{4})\psi_{d}(q_{5})\phi(q_{6})\times\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})e^{-S_{quad}}

\displaystyle=\int dq_{1}\cdots dq_{6}\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{3}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\mbox{Re}W^{cd}_{3}(q_{4},q_{5},q_{6})

(202)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\delta(\vec{q}_{1}-\vec{p}_{2})}{\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{aj}(q_{1},p_{2})}\frac{\delta(\vec{q}_{2}-\vec{q}_{4})}{\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{bc}(q_{2},q_{4})}\frac{\delta(\vec{q}_{5}-\vec{p}_{1})}{\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{id}(q_{5},p_{1})}\times\frac{\delta(\vec{q}_{3}-\vec{p}_{3})}{W_{2}^{\phi}(q_{3},p_{3})}\frac{\delta(\vec{q}_{6}-\vec{p}_{4})}{W_{2}^{\phi}(q_{6},p_{4})}

\displaystyle=\int dq_{2}\frac{\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{3}(p_{2},q_{2},p_{3})\mbox{Re}W^{cd}_{3}(q_{2},p_{1},p_{4})}{\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{aj}(p_{2},p_{2})\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{bc}(q_{2},q_{2})\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{di}(p_{1},p_{1})W_{2}^{\phi}(p_{4},p_{4})W_{2}^{\phi}(p_{3},p_{3})}

Note that the momentum conservations in
W_{3}
would give
\vec{p}_{2}+\vec{p}_{3}+\vec{q}_{2}=\vec{p}_{1}+\vec{p}_{4}+\vec{q}_{2}=0
which would eliminate the
q_{2}
integral. The second term’s contribution to the correlator is given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\braket{\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\bar{\psi}^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})}^{(2)}=\int dq_{1}\cdots dq_{4}\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{4}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4})\\
&\qquad\times\int D\phi D\bar{\psi}D\psi\bar{\psi}_{a}(q_{1})\psi_{b}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\phi(q_{4})\times\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})e^{-S_{quad}}\\
&=\frac{\mbox{Re}W^{ac}_{4}(p_{2},p_{1},p_{3},p_{4})}{\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{aj}(p_{1},p_{1})\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{ci}(p_{2},p_{2})W_{2}^{\phi}(p_{3},p_{3})W_{2}^{\phi}(p_{4},p_{4})}\end{split}

(203)

By adding the two we obtain the full correlator,

\displaystyle\braket{\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{aj}(p_{1},p_{1})\mbox{Re}W_{2}^{di}(p_{2},p_{2})W_{2}^{\phi}(p_{3},p_{3})W_{2}^{\phi}(p_{4},p_{4})}

(204)

\displaystyle\times\left\{\frac{\mbox{Re}W^{ab}_{3}(p_{2},q_{2},p_{3})\mbox{Re}W^{cd}_{3}(q_{2},p_{1},p_{4})}{W_{2}^{bc}(q_{2},q_{2})}+\mbox{Re}W^{ac}_{4}(p_{2},p_{1},p_{3},p_{4})\right\}

One-Loop:
\bm{O(\lambda^{4})}

We can similarly compute the contribution at the next order. It is seen that
O(\lambda^{3})
the contribution is zero. However at
O(\lambda^{4})
receives contributions from multiple Witten diagrams, including

\displaystyle\begin{split}W_{3}^{(1)ab}&=\scalebox{0.75}{\hbox to115.01pt{\vbox to115.01pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 57.50551pt\lower-57.50551pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

(205)

The 4-pt function at
O(\lambda^{4})
is given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\braket{\Psi|\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})|\Psi}=-\int D\psi D\bar{\psi}D\phi\bar{\psi}^{i}(p_{1})\psi^{j}(p_{2})\phi(p_{3})\phi(p_{4})\\
&\times\Bigg\{\int W_{6}^{(1)ab}(q_{1},\cdots,q_{6})\bar{\psi}_{a}(q_{1})\psi_{b}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\cdots\phi(q_{6})+\int W_{4}^{(4)ab}(q_{1},\cdots,q_{4})\bar{\psi}_{a}(q_{1})\psi_{b}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\phi(q_{4})\\
&\quad+\int W_{3}^{(1)ab}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\bar{\psi}_{a}(q_{1})\psi_{b}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\int W_{3}^{(3)cd}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\psi_{c}(q_{1})\psi_{d}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\\
&\quad+\int W_{3}^{(1)ab}(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\bar{\psi}_{a}(q_{1})\psi_{b}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\int W_{5}^{(1)cd}(q_{1},\cdots,q_{5})\bar{\psi}_{c}(q_{1})\psi_{d}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\phi(q_{4})\phi(q_{5}),\\
&\quad+\int W_{4}^{(1)ab}(q_{1},\cdots,q_{4})\bar{\psi}_{a}(q_{1})\psi_{b}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\phi(q_{4})\int W_{4}^{(1)cd}(q_{1},\cdots,q_{4})\bar{\psi}_{c}(q_{1})\psi_{d}(q_{2})\phi(q_{3})\phi(q_{4})\Bigg\}\end{split}

(206)

Using the recursion relations it is easily possible to compute the loop integrands in (
205
). For example,
W_{4}^{(4)ab}
is given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}W_{4}^{(4)ab}&=\bar{u}_{1}\Big(\hbox to71.07pt{\vbox to15.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.53577pt\lower-7.83301pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#00FFFF} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}}{{}}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}
}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{{{}{}{{}}

(207)

however performing the integrals requires some new ideas beyond the scope of the paper. These examples show how we can use the tools developed in the main sections to also compute cosmological correlators in perturbation theory. However, given the complexity of the answer above, it is clear that this approach is not suitable for computing loop diagrams beyond the first few orders. It would be interesting to develop alternate tools to compute such objects. For example, using the shadow formalism for spinors
[
107
]
or the in-out formalism for in-in correlators
[
114
]
seem like interesting avenues to explore in the future.
